The Mad (Color) Science Behind Rian Johnson’s New Camera App

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

You shouldn’t ever take a photo of your midterm election ballot — it’s illegal in some states — but, thanks to Rian Johnson and his longtime DP Steve Yedlin, there is now a very cinematic way to take iPhone photos of everything else.

Yedlin has spent more than 20 years as a cinematographer, collaborating closely with Johnson on everything from “Brick” and “The Brothers Bloom” to “The Last Jedi” and the “Knives Out” films. But Yedlin has also spent 20 years delving into the science of color and how filmmakers can better craft imagery through the technical tools available. And now he and his friends are in the business of making new ones. Related Stories 5 Big Challenges in Creating Criterion’s ‘The Complete Kubrick’: From Navigating HDR/4K to Preserving Stanley Kubrick’s Love of Mono Sound Ebenezer’s Venmo and ‘How to Rob a Bank’ ATMs: Studios Are Now Promoting Movies by Just Giving Away Money

Yedlin, Johnson, and color science coder Eric Cameron have been in the process of creating new software for light controls to replace traditional dimmer boards, pixel mapping, and on-screen playback (ie., when actors are looking at phone or computer screens on camera) with something that embraces the world of color-tunable lights and LEDs. But as a side project, they’ve also just launched Chromagraph, an iPhone app that uses Yedlin’s deep, mathematical understanding of color to bypass the iPhone’s default photo processing settings for a more filmic look.

The app project started because Johnson wanted something on his phone that could capture photos the way that Yedlin captures film — using RAW images (ie., everything the sensor captures, not just what’s rendered on a screen) and bypassing the standard iPhone image processing protocols.

Surprisingly, it only took ten weeks to build, test, and refine the app Johnson wanted to a place the team was happy with it — although Yedlin admittedly told IndieWire the process to create Chromagraph was still pretty intense. The cinematographer had done the math to build an image processing look that works for cinema cameras like the Arri ALEXA, but even the newer, pro-level iPhone cameras have sensors so small they’re smol, and they also spread image information out in a different way from how film cameras work.

“ The code values themselves, like the actual numbers in the pixels, alone don’t tell you how bright something’s supposed to be. It’s the code values in the pixels plus this [metadata] evaluation,” Yedlin said of iPhone cameras, before worrying he was being way too technical for a 900-word article. “It’s not like we’re putting different parameters into existing processing math. It’s totally different math for the most part.”

To make a complicated story shorter, then, Yedlin and Cameron couldn’t reprogram the iPhone camera, but they could get it to behave differently in order to bring RAW images (everything the camera sensor captures) into Chromagraph and then do a bunch of different and complicated math to achieve the image processing for the cinematic look that Rian Johnson wanted to be able to pull up at the swipe of a screen.

The main thing that was important to the team to get right and reflect real color science, as opposed to “bullshit cinema color science,” according to Yedlin, was to work from the truth that images that look aesthetically pleasing are not clinical or technical reproductions. They are not photometrically or colorimetrically “correct.”

“What makes a good image is something that looks enough like what it’s representing to be recognizable and then to be beautiful,” Yedlin said. “Even when you have a very clinical-looking, like a broadcast look as opposed to a cinema look, it’s also not photometrically or colorimetrically correct. They’re just not correct in a different way, you know? It’s always a matter of taste.”

A lot of the very complicated math that went into how Chromagraph does image processing is based on Yedlin and Johnson’s taste for how to make an image look artful, but then the app has contrast and saturation tools for individual photographers to take the look of their work even further. The tools inside of Chromagraph are, in fact, meant to be exactly like the math of white balance in ISO on a film camera.

“It sort of gets repeated as giving photos the ‘Knives Out’ look, and that’s fine if people want to say that, but I don’t think of it that way. I think of it as generally artful rendering. Every single movie made with traditional film stocks was made with one of like three negatives and one of two print stocks, and they all have completely different looks based on the craftsmanship and the photography and what’s being photographed and lighting and everything,” Yedlin said. “This is just the image processing, so it’s one look for that part, but the image processing part isn’t the only thing that makes a photo.”

Where Chromagraph can really help photographers make their own photos, though, is in capturing color in a way that the iPhone default method can’t quite reach. “Vibrant colors is a place where I think Chromagraph really shows off. A lot of times, with garish colors, you can have that saturation clamped look where one of the three channels is slammed so the entire area kinda looks like it’s been just colored in like a coloring book,” Yedlin said. “So some people are posting photos [from the app] where the colors really stand out, but then somebody else is posting photos with the most subtle, nuanced colors. It’s only one look, but it’s for that last little bit of finesse for whatever kind of photo you take.”

For that reason, Yedlin, Johnson, and Cameron don’t necessarily want to support multiple looks inside of Chromagraph. They’re also not planning on building an Android version anytime soon, and while they’d like to support video, that’s still a long way from being worked out. But for photographers with an iPhone in their pocket, the app is a fun, easy shortcut past importing photos into professional post tools.

“It’s satisfying that every snapshot looks the way that we like it,” Yedlin said.

Chromagraph is now available in the Apple App Store.

Is Your Dog Weird Enough for Times Square?

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

We just sent a welcome email your way with a quick overview of what to expect (including those vital recall alerts).

Dogster is reader-supported. When you buy via links on our site, we may earn a commission at no cost to you. Read about privacy.

Does your pup have a bedtime routine that makes no sense, a suspicious obsession with household objects, or dramatically howl at their own reflection in the oven door? Congratulations. You might be living with a contender for the World's Weirdest Dog. Embark Veterinary has officially launched its search for canine oddballs, inviting pet parents to submit photos and videos of their furballâs most gloriously bizarre behaviors before November 15, 2026. The winning pup will earn the ultimate claim to canine fame: their face on a Times Square billboard, the official title of World's Weirdest Dog, and a $200 gift card.

The contest celebrates the behaviors that make dogs funny, unique, and truly memorable; the ones that make us smile when we think about them, while also highlighting  Embark's Behavior Insights & Training feature, which explores how canine DNA may influence behavior.

Sock Thieves, Bathtub Sleepers, and Other Household Oddballs

Furry weirdos have interesting, different habits; Embark's examples include a dog who sleeps upside down in the bathtub, one who carries a sock around without chewing it, and a canine who spins exactly three times before lying down. Not two. Not four. Three.

Then there's the dog who refuses to walk past one particular house, or the dinner diva who won't touch a meal until someone has given it the full restaurant treatment.

Such behaviors are entertaining partly because they are so unique and specific. Most pet parents can share at least one story that would make a perfectly reasonable person wonder what goes on inside that furry little head.

A sock might be a very treasured object, a source of comfort, a reminder of their favorite personâs scent, or simply something fun to carry. A particular walking route might feel uncomfortable for reasons a person cannot detect, most likely a scentâ¦ but then again, who knows? And a bedtime ritual may have started with nothing more mysterious than finding a comfortable position.

Of course, some dogs are just little weirdos, and that's part of their charm. But there's a difference between a pup who has always slept with their legs in the air and one who suddenly starts acting strangely. If your pup begins staring at walls, repeating the same movements, or turning down food when that's not normal for them, don't automatically label it as a new amusing personality trait. Pain, illness, and anxiety can all show up as changes in behavior. If something feels off, put the camera down and call the vet.

Genes can influence behavioral tendencies, but they don't write a complete script for an individual dog's life. Learning and environment play a big role, too, sometimes in ways pet parents never intended.

Take the sock thief. A dog grabs a sock, their pet parents react with dramatic excitement, and suddenly the household is involved in a thrilling chase. From our perspective, it's a laundry crime. From the pup's perspective, it may be an excellent new game.

Behaviors that lead to positive or rewarding outcomes are more likely to happen again. Attention, play, food, and access to preferred activities can all reinforce behavior, even when nobody is training anything on purpose.

Genetics can contribute, too. Inherited tendencies may influence a dog's motivation to chase, retrieve, explore, or seek food. But a predisposition doesn't guarantee a particular behavior, nor does it explain every individual habit. A dog's behavior comes from a combination of biology and learned experience, which is often less convenient than blaming everything on their breed, but it is much closer to reality.

A 2022 study published in Science looked at the behavior and DNA of thousands of dogs. Researchers found that a dog's breed explained only about 9% of the differences in behavior between individual dogs. In other words, dogs of the same breed can have very different personalities and behaviors. Genetics plays a role, but it doesn't tell the whole story.

That doesn't mean genetics is irrelevant. The researchers found evidence that many behavioral traits do have a heritable component. Rather, the findings show why breed alone is a poor crystal ball for predicting how an individual dog will behave.

Some dogs may be especially motivated to retrieve, chase, or investigate, reflecting tendencies shaped partly by their ancestry and breeding history. But individual dogs still vary, and experience helps shape how those tendencies appear. However, with more data, we may learn new things; science is ever evolving along with our technologies.

Embark's Behavior Insights & Training feature connects genetic information with behavioral insights and training guidance. Genetic results can be useful, but they are one piece of the puzzle and not a personality prediction.

Behavior is best understood in context. Has the habit been present for years, or did it start last week? What happens before the behavior? Could pain, fear, stress, or another medical issue be contributing?

If a pup is safe and comfortable, there's often no reason to stop a peculiar routine unless itâs a problem. A dog who carries a favorite toy around the house doesn't need a corrective training plan. When a behavior does create a problem, redirection and positive reinforcement can help teach an alternative response without relying on intimidation or punishment. This is the science of behavioral modification.

The goal isn't to turn every pup into a perfectly polished obedience champion, but to help us understand what each dog needs and help them become great family members.

Pet parents can enroll their weirdos through November 15, 2026, by uploading a photo or video of their pup's strangest behavior, with an optional short story, at Embark's official contest page. No weird dog? No problem. You can still get involved by casting your vote for the weirdest dog through the official voting page.

The first-place winner will receive the World's Weirdest Dog title, a Times Square billboard feature, and a $200 gift card. Second through fifth place will receive $150 gift cards each, while sixth through fifteenth place will receive an Embark Breed + Health Dog DNA Test with the Behavior Insights & Training upgrade.

So, if you know your furry pal is a weirdo, this may be their moment. Let that freak flag fly!

Canva is announcing changes and layoffs affecting 22 percent of its staff

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Canva is making significant changes affecting 1,271 people, which is 22 percent of its staff, co-founder and CEO Melanie Perkins announced today. “For some people, this will mean changes to their roles or being asked to relocate to centralised hubs,” says Perkins. “Sadly, it will also mean saying goodbye to teammates and friends who have helped build Canva into what it is today.”

After exploring a wide range of alternatives, I’ve come to the difficult conclusion that small changes won’t be enough. To set ourselves up for the next decade, we need to refound Canva, getting back to our roots of small, empowered teams with the freedom to make decisions, take ownership and focus on what matters most for our community.

Canva’s changes include bringing its Product, Growth, and Platform teams together into one product organization, “simplifying layers” and “consolidating overlapping responsibilities,” and making Sydney the “primary home for more of our product teams, as well as roles in other Specialties where shared context and close collaboration are especially important.” Starting in January, Canva will also “expect” most hybrid employees to spend 2 to 3 days in an office each week “depending on what makes sense in your area.”

Employees leaving Canva will get benefits including at least six months of salary, twelve months of healthcare coverage, and career support resources.

Yesterday, Squarespace announced layoffs affecting 19 percent of its staff.

“Love to the Point of Invention” – Injured Kitten with a Tiny Toy-Car Wheelchair Finds His Forever Home

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Catster is reader-supported. When you buy via links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission at no cost to you. Learn more.

Some kittens steal your heart with their big eyes. Others win you over with their playful antics. And then there's Gelato, a tiny kitten who needed a wheelchair and ended up getting one made from Hot Wheels cars. Yes, really. Human ingenuity and humane creativity to the rescue!

Becoming more mobile in a Hot Wheels wheelchair might seem like a dream come true, but today, Gelato has even more reasons to celebrate: Heâs found his forever home.

Gelato arrived at Cincinnati Animal CARE in Ohio as a stray kitten with a lower-back injury that left him unable to use his hind legs normally. The shelter team wanted to help him move around, but there was a rather inconvenient problem. The wheelchairs they had available were too big for his tiny body.

Now, anyone who has lived with a kitten knows that, besides being irresistibly cute, they can be remarkably determined little creatures. They want to explore everything, investigate every corner, and get involved in whatever their humans are doing. Being unable to move around easily can limit those everyday experiences for the tiny fur balls.

Fortunately, Gelato had people in his corner who weren't about to let his small size stop him from exploring the big world.

The shelter's lead veterinary technician, Mallory Smith, came up with a wonderfully resourceful solution. She built a miniature wheelchair using cardboard, tape, zip ties, wooden rulers, and Hot Wheels toy cars. The first version needed adjustments, but Mallory kept working until the little device was ready to support Gelato as he moved around. While this is certainly quite a unique solution, what isnât unusual is the compassion and creativity behind it. Gelatoâs improvised mobility device is a perfect example of how animal medical care personnel go above and beyond to help patients in need.

Gelato's homemade wheelchair was a clever solution to an immediate problem. However, a makeshift device is not necessarily the best long-term option for a growing kitten with mobility challenges.

Help arrived in the form of an engineer who created a custom 3D-printed wheelchair designed to fit his body and accommodate his growth. The toy cars were gone, but Gelatoâs future mobility was looking more stable.

Gelato got lucky the moment someone refused to give up and asked, "How can we make this work?" This first lucky chance opened him to new opportunities from a wider effort to help one small animal have a better chance at life. Itâs impressive how sometimes sharing an animal's story can make all the difference. A social media post can connect shelters with people who have the skills, resources, or homes that animals need.

Giving Gelato a way to move around was an important first step. And there was another milestone waiting for him: a loving family of his own.

As the story of Gelatoâs unusual mode of transport spread, drawing plenty of social media attention, it also caught the eye of Hope Hollandsworth, a 28-year-old tattoo artist and devoted cat parent. When she first saw a photograph of Gelato during the summer, she felt immediately drawn to him.

Hollandsworth already cared for two cats with spinal injuries, Bread Crumb and Sprinkles. Both have limited use of their hind legs and rely on their front legs for mobility. She understood that caring for cats with special needs could involve extra responsibilities, adjustments, and plenty of patience.

In other words, she wasn't going into this adoption without knowing that life with a disabled cat can look a little different. Cat lovers are abundant, but those experienced in caring for disabled felines? Not quite as plentiful.

Hope wasted no time, adopting Gelato as soon as she met him at the shelter. The five-month-old kitten spent his first night in his new home sleeping in her arms.

Absolutely. A disability does not automatically mean that a cat has a poor quality of life. Cats with mobility challenges can still enjoy affection, play, exploration, food puzzles, comfortable naps, and the company of people they trust.

What really matters is understanding the individual cat's needs and making reasonable adjustments to help them live comfortably.

For a cat with a spinal injury, this may include providing easy access to food, water, resting areas, and litter boxes. Non-slip flooring, ramps, and thoughtfully arranged furniture can also help some cats move around more safely. Depending on the injury, a cat may need help with bladder or bowel function, regular skin checks, or a rehabilitation plan.

These are not small details. Some cats with spinal injuries cannot empty their bladders normally, which can lead to serious complications without very devoted care. A veterinarian can assess bladder function, mobility, sensation, and other concerns, then explain what support the cat will need at home. The real challenge is finding someone with the time and patience to take over these responsibilities on a daily basis.

Hollandsworth's experience with Bread Crumb and Sprinkles gave her a head start.

Gelato will continue receiving therapy in the hope that he may regain some use of his hind legs, but for now, his recovery is uncertain. Spinal injuries vary, and a cat's prognosis depends on several factors, including the extent of the damage and the neurological function that remains.

But Gelato doesn't need a guaranteed recovery to deserve a happy future. He's got somewhere to go, someone to cuddle, and absolutely no need to prove that he can walk to earn his place in the family.

His new family is ready to love and support him, whether or not he ever walks on his own. Hollandsworth put it beautifully: to her, Gelato is already perfect just the way he is.

There is a lot of wisdom in that simple sentiment. We can hope for progress, celebrate small improvements, and pursue appropriate treatment without making an animal's worth depend on how much they recover. Loving others, whether animals, people, or even ourselves, for who they are, exactly as they are, is what we call true love.

Dr. Paola Cuevas is the Senior In-House Veterinarian at Pangolia, the parent company of Catster. She has over 18 years of experience as a veterinarian and animal behaviorist. Paola's focus is on preventive medicine and animal welfare. Paola has a large range of skills in from diagnostic imaging to nutrition and clinical pathology. Paola enjoys sharing her extensive knowledge of animal training with our Catster readers and is always happy to answer reader questions as our in-house veterinarian.

Bruno Fernandes confession: ‘I was lucky on first penalty, then made a decision’

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Bruno Fernandes explained what went through his mind when retaking that penalty to revive Manchester United with Roma. ‘I was lucky honestly, but the second time I made a decision.’

The Giallorossi had been dominating at Old Trafford in the Champions League this evening, leading 2-0 after 18 minutes with a Matias Soulé strike and Donyell Malen spot-kick.

Manchester United were given the opportunity to get back into it with a soft penalty, but Bruno Fernandes saw his effort parried by Devis Vasquez.

As Bryan Cristante encroached to make the clearance on the rebound, VAR ruled it had to be retaken, and this time Bruno Fernandes converted to start the comeback, eventually winning 3-2.

“I knew it was a big responsibility, it could’ve changed the game and we really needed a goal to get the crowd behind us at that moment,” Bruno Fernandes told Sky Sport Italia.

“The penalty is always a stressful moment, because it is just you against the goalkeeper and nobody else can help in that situation.

“I was lucky honestly that Cristante went into the box too early, so it gave me an opportunity to take it again. The second time, I decided to pick a spot and hit it as hard as I could without trying to second-guess where the goalkeeper was going.”

After the 4-1 defeat to Como in the Champions League, it was reported the Manchester United staff refused to let Bruno Fernandes speak to the local media in Italian, even though he spent many years in Serie A and B.

Evidently something changed, because the Portugal international was able to converse in Italian this evening and discussed the current crisis at Manchester United, who had been without a win since September 10.

“It is a very important win, because obviously things aren’t going the way we want them to. We aim to do one thing and we do another, we concede goals at the start of games, it is a truly difficult moment, but we must try to get ourselves out of it,” added the midfielder.

“I hope this can give us the confidence to go forward and do better, because there is quality in this squad, we saw what we did last season and it’s not possible that it all just disappeared.

“These are the same players as before, we have added some good quality over the summer, and we need to keep going.”

Mamdani and others have called out Google's decision to disable search functionality for schools who opt out of its AI tools.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Saying that it believes "schools should have control over AI in their classrooms," Google announced today that it will turn off access to its AI tools for K-12 student accounts. But there's a catch: Along with those AI tools, it will also disable Google search functionality.

In a blog post entitled "Designing responsible AI for learning," Chris Phillips, Google's vice president and general manager of education, extolled the virtues of AI tools in "productive struggle," which he described as "enabling students to be actively challenged without getting so overwhelmed that they give up."

"That's the philosophy behind Guided Learning," Philips wrote. "In Gemini, Guided Learning acts as an interactive study tool that coaches students through the process of problem solving through open-ended questions and step-by-step breakdowns. Our research shows that Guided Learning leads to measurable learning gains for students, and we're now bringing Guided Learning to more products." Those products include Google Search and Google Classroom.

"After listening to schools, teachers, leading researchers, and policymakers, we are now updating our approach to controls for Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search," Phillips continued. "Access to these products will be entirely off for K-12 student accounts until school administrators explicitly turn them on."

It sounds like Google Search on student accounts can be re-enabled separately, but that also ushers AI back into the equation: Google said in the update that it is "building Guided Learning capabilities into Search for student accounts to help them work through problems without skipping straight to answers." In other words, there is no search functionality on these accounts without AI intrusion.

Speaking to the New York Times, Phillips said "search and AI are deeply integrated," and confirmed that schools can't have one without the other: Students at schools that have turned off Google's AI tools "will not have access to search, and they'll see a message from their school that indicates it's not available."

Phillips presents the policy as responsiveness and responsibility on Google's part, but the reaction from educators is not entirely positive. American Federation of Teachers president Randi Weingarten told NBC that "Google has prioritized its business interests over the education of our children," adding, "Instead of working with teachers and parents to uphold privacy and ensure their tools are safe and useful, it throws the baby out with the bathwater and bans access to the tools altogether."

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

Fairplay, a non-profit that aims to protect children from "the exploitative and harmful business practices of marketers and Big Tech," was somewhat harsher in its assessment. "Google's attempt to strong-arm schools into using its AI products is likely to backfire," Fairplay executive director Josh Golin said in a statement. "Schools should use this opportunity not only to turn off AI, but to teach students that 'Google' isn't a verb, but the name of a surveillance and advertising company whose presence in schools has nothing to do with learning and everything to do with developing lifetime brand loyalty.

"Because AI isn't safe for kids and undermines education and critical thinking, schools should turn off Google's AI tools the minute that option becomes available. And rather than fret about losing access to Google search, schools should use this opportunity to help students find more privacy-protective alternatives for search, as well as ways to do more substantive research beyond search and click."

Former FTC chair Lina Khan, who was right about Microsoft's acquisition of Activision Blizzard, also weighed in, accusing Google of "using its search monopoly to coerce schools into adopting AI."

"Multiple courts have found Google liable for violating antitrust laws, and this latest move shows it remains unchastened," Khan wrote on X. "As the public increasingly pushes back against the use of AI in schools, Google is making clear it will punish those who don't fall in line."

New York City mayor Zohran Mamdani, a longstanding critic of oligopolies and corporate greed, said that "Google is trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools."

Google is trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools. No corporation should have the power to dictate how we teach our children.  Our public schools exist to serve students, not to grow the profit margins of Big Tech.

As NBC noted, research on the impact of AI in education is still quite limited, and some studies purporting to show meaningful benefits have been found to be flawed. That makes the headlong rush into AI integration, championed by massive AI companies and pretty much no one else, so controversial and potentially dangerous. Trying to force AI into classrooms while the technology's impact on mental health is an increasingly serious concern is audacious, and promises of more rigorous safety standards tend to ring hollow anyway: In October, the attorneys general for eight US states accused competing product OpenAI of ignoring its own safety warnings in pursuit of profit.

Google's new policy on AI in schools comes just days after shares in Google parent company Alphabet took a hammering as the company's AI Mode cut deep into its search business and revenue. I've reached out to Google for more information about the new educational policy and will update if I receive a reply.

Why the Algerian Sahara Should Be Your Next Off-Grid Adventure

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

I woke up in the small hours of the night. Orion’s Belt was directly above where I lay on a thin roll-up mattress, zipped into a canvas sleeping bag. The hunter’s three luminous studs stood out in a canopy awash with stars from horizon to horizon, the Milky Way a glittery paint stroke across the scene. Aside from an occasional shuffle or snuffle from my fellow campers, the night was draped in a thick blanket of stress-shrinking quiet. It’d been days since I had cell reception. I could practically hear my nervous system thanking me.

A breathtaking canopy of stars illuminates the vast Algerian Sahara.

You don’t need to sleep al fresco while camping in the Sahara Desert—I could crawl back inside my own pop-up dome tent at any time—but I’d certainly recommend it. It was a highlight of my trip to Algeria’s tranche of this continent-spanning desert.

I was in the Sahara on a nine-day tour with Explore Worldwide, a UK-based small-group tour company, which began running trips to Algeria earlier this year, partly as a reaction to the country’s new, albeit cautious, encouragement of tourism. I wanted to find out what that development looked like on the ground and experience the desert region that hopes to attract new visitors.

Algeria is one of our Best Places to Go in Africa in 2026. Find our full guide here.

Africa’s largest country is hard to miss on a map, but all but one of the 10 well-traveled people in my group (mostly British and 60-years-plus) were first-timers in Algeria. Traditionally, the economy here has been driven by the oil and gas sector, and the development of tourism has been of little interest to the government. This has led to a fraction of the arriving tourists received by other North African countries such as Morocco or Egypt; roughly a third of its visitors are members of the diaspora coming to visit and stay with family. But recently, a push to diversify the economy has led to the launch of an ambitious “master plan” to increase arrivals four-fold by 2030, with a special focus on desert tourism.

Although the majority of projects (such as much-needed new hotels) remain in-progress, the government has introduced visas on arrival for people spending the majority of their time in the Sahara—an option taken by nearly 39,000 visitors since 2023, contributing to an uptick in arrivals. I took advantage of the scheme myself. After an hour or so in the capital Algiers’ Houari Boumediene International Airport—queuing, waiting, and nervously watching an official wander around with my passport—I paid my fee in cash ($150 for 10 days), my passport was stamped, and I was warmly welcomed to Algeria.

Algiers' iconic Casbah is the most ancient quarter of the city—and well worth exploring for its winding alleys and traditional Ottoman-style architecture.

The trip kicked off with an Algiers city tour, giving us a brief but necessary taste of the country’s culture and history before we flew into the desert. I arrived early for a little solo exploration, allowing me to wander the cool, peaceful paths of the lush Hamma Botanical Garden, and enjoy a bowl of rechta (thin noodles with chicken in a clear broth) in the intimate lounge of El Walima Restaurant.

With our tour guide Wassim Messgo, we walked through the narrow lanes of the Casbah—the UNESCO-listed historic center—past a smattering of souvenir stalls, and visited the hard-hitting National Museum of the Mujahid—dedicated to the bloody war for independence from France between 1954 and 1962. I could’ve spent longer in Algiers and want to return to see other cities in the north. Messgo recommended Constantine: “You get Roman ruins in other parts of the region,” he said, “but not in a city like this.”

But my focus for this trip was the Sahara.

The Algerian government has introduced visas on arrival for people spending the majority of their time in the Sahara.

Algiers to Djanet—a little oasis town in Algeria’s southeast near the Libya and Niger borders—is a two-hour flight, usually scheduled in the middle of the night (to avoid desert winds during the day, so I was told). On arrival, I was reminded of Algeria’s high levels of security, partly due to fluctuating tensions with neighboring countries, particularly Morocco at present. Our passports were collected and our names ticked off against a list that linked us with our guide: Aissa Khirani, a Djanet native who’s spent the past 20 years leading tours locally and across Africa. Khirani told me that guides have to submit their plans to the military, including the identities of guests and staff, along with car registration numbers.

In addition to being a military hub, Djanet is the tourism gateway to this swathe of the Sahara. Our flight was perhaps half tourists—but for the five days we were in the desert, we encountered only six other vehicles. This was a benefit of traveling with a guide who knew his way around, while having a flexible itinerary so we could avoid other travelers—and account for unexpected weather events.

Djanet is a little oasis town in Algeria’s southeast near the Libya and Niger borders, and a gateway to the Sahara.

As we drove around the Tassili n'Ajjer plateau over the following five days, the landscape varied with surprising frequency—Mars-like rocky flats; sandy canyons edged by colossal rock formations; sweeping seas of peachy dunes with crisp ridgelines and wind-rippled faces. Camels, many belonging to Touareg nomads, grazed on sparse patches of vegetation. Caves and overhangs here have protected at least 15,000 examples of rock art, some thought to be 10,000 years old, depicting people hunting or dancing, giraffes, cattle, and jars possibly for carrying water as the region dried up.

We eased into desert life with two nights at something close to a glamping site—shared with a couple of other guests—which had tents you could stand up in, thick mattresses, even charging units. For the following three nights, we camped among the dunes and at the base of hulking rockfaces that resembled elephant skin. Our mobile set-up consisted of basic tents, a dining table by the campfire, and a “bathroom”—a chair with a cut-out seat in a canvas cubicle.

Camping at the base of the hulking rockfaces of the Algerian Sahara

Tents come with comfortable mattresses, extra seating, even charging units

“To be honest, I don't want Algeria to be like Morocco or Tunisia or Egypt,” Khirani told me, as we drove out of Djanet in a convoy of four Toyota four-wheel-drives, echoing sentiments Messgo had also shared. Both guides spoke about how relaxed Algeria currently feels for visitors; when I visited Djanet’s market, for example, we’d been greeted but otherwise left alone to browse the stalls of jewelry and fabrics.

As for the desert, Khirani continued, it still feels remote. “The desert to me means peace of mind,” he said. “It’s a sensation like emptiness, but with it a kind of happiness that you cannot describe.” He hopes to protect that experience, while encouraging tourism “in a smart way.”

The shifting shades and surfaces of the desert dunes

Caves and overhangs in the region have protected at least 15,000 examples of rock art, some thought to be 10,000 years old.

While I had precious moments of solitude—sitting on a dune at sunrise, submerged in quietness as the sand turned cool beige to warm caramel—many of my favorite memories are of people: Hemaya baking bread in the sand under hot coals; driver Ali Lazghem playing the same four songs on repeat and letting other drivers know exactly how he felt when they stalled at the top of a dune he was powering up; the whole team excited to see a locally famous and yet unnamed rock formation that features on the cover of Lonely Planet’s latest Algeria guide for the first time.

Back in Djanet, at the end of our off-grid adventure, I was finally able to shower again. I knew one wash wouldn’t be enough to fully de-grit my hair—at that stage, almost solid with desert debris—and that I’d be tapping out orange sand from my shoes for days. But that’s the thing about time in the desert. It tends to stick with you, in more ways than one.

I traveled with Explore Worldwide on their nine-day Algeria’s Sahara Desert Adventure tour, which begins and ends in the capital Algiers. Prices start at $2,410 (excluding international flights, which are typically via hubs such as Paris or London). The package includes accommodation, most meals and activities, including a day-tour of Algiers and five nights camping in the Sahara Desert; don’t expect guides to keep strictly to the itinerary, but you’ll certainly spend plenty of time in scenic parts of the Sahara, see several examples of rock art, and be treated to warm hospitality and tasty food throughout. If you want to explore the northern cities too, you can do Explore’s two-week Ancient Algeria and the Sahara Desert tour. In Algiers, the comfortable El Aurassi is considered one of the best hotels, though service and style fall short of a standard five-star experience. For a visa on arrival, tour operators will provide the required authorization letter—otherwise you can apply for a visa from your nearest Algerian embassy. Bring dollars to exchange for Algerian dinars when you arrive (ATMs are unreliable), as cards aren’t commonly accepted. Exporting dinars is prohibited. Also note that it’s illegal to carry binoculars and drones into Algeria.

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

Martin Drury accuses Huddersfield Town of looking for 'perfect goal' in display full of character

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

A sloppy moment defensively cancelled out by Joe Taylor's stunning volley but not added to meant Town had to settle for a 1-1 draw in a game when they had lots of the ball in their attacking third, but did not make enough of it.

Coach Drury felt their mindset was slightly to blame.

"A challenging game, a difficult game against a team with really good individuals. I thought they started the game slightly better than us, started a little bit quicker. We'd been scoring goals early on in the game, so it was disappointing to start as slow as we did.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

"That being said, I thought we started to understand the game better, understand what they were doing better and how we could attack them. I thought we then started to take control.

"I thought we were absolutely dominant in the first half, got to their box numerous times, hit the post, hit the bar.

"We probably just lacked that little bit of decision-making in key moments for us. I felt we were trying to almost find the perfect angle to shoot tonight and not use their defenders to help us, whether it be through the legs or shape one round in.

"We were looking for a little bit more perfection and clear-cut chances, but Luton defended well.

"We get to half-time, it threw up a new challenge for us. We've not been behind at half-time this season in the league. That was a new challenge for us, which I'd welcome because I want to know what we're made of in those moments when it's not all our own way.

"In the second half, I thought we started really well. We were strong, we were dominant, scored a fantastic goal. From there on, I thought we were in the ascendancy and I thought we'd go on to win.

"But you're playing against good players and a good group. The game then started to settle down again.

"I thought we had some really nice attacking moments. Luton had one or two maybe half-chances, but we got into their box so many times, we just lacked that killer instinct in the final third.

"But, again, take nothing away from them, they defended the box really well."

Luton led against the run of play through former Barnsley player Jordan Clark after San Roughan overcommitted at a long ball and Huddersfield missed a couple of chances to clear their lines.

"I'm really disappointed with the goal," said Drury. "The goal is a very, very poor goal to concede.

"We have to do better in that moment because no disrespect, but that is not a well-worked goal. That is a goal that we've not defended well enough.

"We've gambled on the ball in behind and then we've not headed it clear. We're in the middle of the box and then we've come to block the shot and it's one player against two and they get shot and then scored. So, that's not a goal that we would normally concede.

"What I did like is the reaction after it.

"Both as individual players and the group as a whole, I thought there was a real strong mentality there to kick on from there. And, as I say, second half, I thought we showed that a lot. But disappointing, just not to kick on and get any more goals."

Ubisoft are reportedly tapped as one potential company that could acquire Arkane, though the Dishonored studio's own management may be in the running too

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

I'd really like it if it weren't Ubisoft though

Things are potentially looking up for Arkane, depending on how you feel about Ubisoft (and depending on who actually ends up acquiring them). I mention the Assassin's Creed developer because, reportedly, they're interested in buying the studio from Microsoft, having even made a bid for it. I'll go out on a limb here and say it's the whole being French thing, but uh, I'd really rather someone else bought Arkane.

This comes from, you guessed it, Bloomberg, who say that sources they've spoken with claim two other gaming companies have also made a bit. There is even the potential that Arkane would be sold to their own management, as Xbox are considering such a move. But overall, it doesn't yet sound like anything is leaning any particular way, as Xbox haven't formalised a deal with any of the potential buyers. Bloomberg also note that if a deal is made, this should then allow Arkane to finish their work on their Blade game, which is expected to release within the next couple of years.

Apparently an Xbox spokesperson told Bloomberg that the gaming company is exploring their options ahead of their December deadline, but didn't comment any further. Ubisoft reportedly did not have a comment to make on the matter. For some context on that December deadline, because of French labour laws, companies that have over 1000 staff members are required to search for a buyer for three months before they can just completely shut down. That makes December 13th the final date that such a deal has to be made by, meaning there's about a month and a half left to go.

Arkane's sale comes as part of a sweeping range of cuts, sales and closures being made by Microsoft at the moment. In July, it was announced that studios like Double Fine, Undead Labs and Compulsion would no longer be a part of Xbox, with all three going indie; Ninja Theory was also announced as part of these cuts, but unfortunately it's looking like they'll be shutting their doors. Thousands of Xbox staff were also laid off at this time, with more laid off in September, and even more coming this month and January reportedly. As always, solidarity with all workers affected by Microsoft's heartless and money hungry cuts.

Tether Holds 150,000 Bitcoin, CEO Says—About 50,000 More Than Its Reserve Report Shows

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Tether Holds 150,000 Bitcoin, CEO Says—About 50,000 More Than Its Reserve Report Shows

Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino said the stablecoin giant now holds around 150,000 Bitcoin, worth roughly $11.5 billion at current prices, a figure that exceeds what the company’s own reserve disclosures imply by some 50,000 coins.

“We have give or take 150,000 bitcoin ourselves. It’s not much, but it’s honest work,” Ardoino said during a keynote at the Plan ₿ Forum in Lugano, Switzerland, the city where Tether has run a Bitcoin and stablecoin adoption program since 2022.

The claim is notable because it doesn’t square neatly with the paper trail. Tether’s third-quarter reserve attestation, which covers the assets backing its USDT stablecoin, lists $8.37 billion of Bitcoin valued at $83,621 per coin, implying roughly 100,060 BTC.

That leaves about 50,000 coins, worth close to $3.8 billion, that its public filings do not yet account for. Ardoino did not say where the additional Bitcoin is held or when it was acquired, so his comment doesn’t indicate a fresh purchase of that size, and Tether has long maintained that its corporate Bitcoin investments sit separately from the reserves supporting USDT.

The stablecoin issuer has been accumulating Bitcoin for years. In May 2023, Tether said it would funnel up to 15% of its net realized operating profits into Bitcoin, and that it would custody the coins itself rather than rely on third-party custodians.

The strategy has been fueled by staggering profits: the company reported $1.5 billion in net operating profit in the third quarter alone, and it has posted billion-dollar quarterly profits throughout the year while its USDT supply has kept growing.

The broader reserve picture, as laid out in the Q3 report, shows $191.45 billion in assets against $183.50 billion in liabilities, leaving an excess cushion of $7.95 billion, nearly double the $4.11 billion buffer it reported in June. U.S. Treasury bills remain the bulk of reserves at about $132.4 billion.

The transparency gap is a sensitive subject for Tether, which has faced years of scrutiny over whether USDT is fully backed. The company began its first full financial audit with KPMG this year, a long-awaited step toward addressing those doubts.

Ardoino also used the Lugano stage to tout Tether’s scale, saying the company now serves more than 730 million users worldwide and is adding roughly 30 million wallets a quarter, as he pitched a suite of open-source technologies funded by its profits.

Sebastien Pocognoli's Scotland report card has stark message as Euro 2028 qualification warning issued

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Just a few days after the last international window concluded with a deeply disappointing 2-1 defeat to Slovenia, Sebastien Pocognoli was back at Hampden.

Nothing too surprising about that, really. After all, he is the Scotland manager. He is, though, based in Belgium and he admitted he needed some time back in his homeland with his family to "refuel" after a challenging and sometimes fraught opening period in charge.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

But he was back in Scotland soon enough to attend a review of his first fixtures in charge, which saw his team take just four Nations League points from a possible 12 against Slovenia (twice), North Macedonia and Switzerland. Although it would have been good to be a fly on the wall on Hampden’s sixth floor as Pocognoli endured this assessment exercise, it’s easy to imagine the gist of the early report card: Must do better.

Pocognoli himself recognises this. “We need to improve,” he said on Monday at the national stadium after naming his second squad. “The first camp was to get to know each other.

“I saw things in training and in the games we produced some very good moments. We created a lot of chances, maybe more than in previous years against those types of opponents. But the next step is to score more goals and clean up the mistakes we made because we conceded goals from nothing. We must try to be consistent in the way we want to play.

“We need to improve but then every country who started with a new coach in September would say the same. Many players in the first camp took it as a new experience with a new style of play and system. That needs a little bit of time but it doesn’t mean we can’t now ask for more and reach the level we want.”

Pocognoli will be handed more time whatever happens in the next two games against North Macedonia and Switzerland. Perhaps the most important date left this year is 6 December, when the draw for Euro 2028 takes place in Belfast. Despite a potentially damaging assumption that Scotland have already qualified as one of the hosts, there’s still work to be done. “We are not qualified,” stressed the Belgian. “We need to qualify. The best way is to qualify from our group.”

He mentioned the need to “stimulate” a winning habit as quickly as possible so that when and if qualification is secured, reaching the knockout stage at a major finals – the final frontier as far as Scotland are concerned – is easier to achieve.

This will go down well with the Hampden top brass. While those first four games will have been robustly scrutinised, it’s only fair Pocognoli’s bosses apply some context to their findings beyond the fact that the heavy defeat against Switzerland was defined by an early red card for Jack Hendry. Pocognoli has barely had time to get his loafers under the desk.

He was also badly served by circumstances. On top of the absent Scott McTominay, recovering following a minor heart procedure, Kieran Tierney and Che Adams were unavailable due to injury. Pocognoli has called up all three for the upcoming games, in the process adding 30 goals to a squad that looked severely short in this department last time out.

Then there was the understandable pomp and ceremony involved in Andy Robertson reaching 100 caps. It meant an early invite back for Steve Clarke, who will have found it excruciating to be perceived as peering over Pocognoli’s shoulder as Scotland were outplayed on their own turf by Slovenia. It was an awkward night, off the pitch as well as on it.

Clarke is actually back in Glasgow next week as a guest of honour at the Street Soccer gala dinner. It will be surprising if he decides to attend Scotland’s home match against North Macedonia two days later. He knows Pocognoli needs space and time to continue trying to put his own mark on the role.

“It was a tough camp,” Pocognoli acknowledged, with reference to those first post-Clarke games. “It was an intense camp. I really liked it. But in terms of intensity…..four games, and I needed to get to know everybody – like 50, 60 new faces. Plus all you (journalists) - 70! It was intense.

“I needed to rest a little bit and refuel after the camp. After one week I was already back here for the review of the camp with the staff and the board and now I am here all this week to prepare for the next camp.”

The Belgian needs to familiarise himself with one more new face: on-loan Rangers player James Penrice, who has been recruited to replace the injured Josh Doig. The squad looks infinitely stronger than last time around thanks chiefly to the inclusion of McTominay and also Adams, still reckoned by many observers – this writer included - to be Scotland’s best striker. The Torino forward's presence takes some of the pressure off Oli McBurnie, who is still struggling to score his first Scotland goal but has struck in his last two appearances for Hull City.

Surprisingly, Aaron Hickey has not started for Brentford since before the last international window. On the upside, Ben Gannon-Doak has seen more action than he is used to in a club context - just as Pocognoli predicted when speaking prior to the last Slovenia game.

The manager was addressing the likely negative impact another period of limited playing time for his club would have on the youngster, who started two of Scotland’s four games in the last window. Pocognoli ventured that, because of Bournemouth’s European commitments, Gannon-Doak will be required to play a more meaningful part as the season progresses, and so it has proved.

The 20-year-old winger was even man of the match in the recent 3-0 Europa League win over AC Milan. Many pointed to this result as underlining football’s new world order. Scotland, meanwhile, have dropped one place to 43 in the international rankings since they last played. They need to start going back up the way without delay.

5 Big Challenges in Creating Criterion’s ‘The Complete Kubrick’: From Navigating HDR/4K to Preserving Stanley Kubrick’s Love of Mono Sound

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

In 2024, archivist Michael Chaiken was approached by the Criterion Collection about what was being described as a “big Kubrick project.” It would start with remastering the director’s last film, “Eyes Wide Shut,” but for months the scope of the ambitious project remained an open question: Was a complete Stanley Kubrick box set even possible?

Kubrick was a precise director, and his control over every aspect of his filmmaking process extended to its home video presentation. But home video technology has developed in ways (widescreen TVs, high dynamic range, 4K resolution, Dolby Atmos) that Kubrick could never have imagined before he died in 1999. As a result — from the image to sound to design of the box itself — there was the underlying question driving Chaiken and the team’s work: “What would Stanley do?” Related Stories Ebenezer’s Venmo and ‘How to Rob a Bank’ ATMs: Studios Are Now Promoting Movies by Just Giving Away Money What Are Local Movie Theaters Showing on Election Night?

“I had to research the history of Stanley Kubrick on home video, and that’s a very specific thing,” said Chaiken. “It’s almost like for a biographer that would be [an important] footnote.”

In an interview with IndieWire, Chaiken broke down the challenges and key work behind “The Complete Kubrick,” which hit stores and mailboxes last week.

Home video fanatics are often completists. And if you are going to invest the time in a $599.95 box set (on sale for 30 percent off at most retailers), the motivation to make it comprehensive and definitive is obvious, but with a career spanning five decades, that isn’t always possible.

“You have to kick around the idea, ‘What does ‘complete’ mean? And how would we make that so?,” said Chaiken.

The heart of Kubrick’s feature film career has been well preserved. His final five films were made with Warner Bros., and the studio’s archive team has kept the original elements well preserved. Meanwhile, Criterion, back in its laser disc era, in the 1980s and ’90s, had a good working relationship with Kubrick himself (more about that later) and was already familiar with what was available (and already in-house) when it came to having to remaster his key films into the HDR/4K standards of today.

“It’s the early stuff that was tricky because he wasn’t working for a studio; he was an independent,” said Chaiken. “[A complete box set] almost didn’t happen; it was really at the 11th hour that it came together.”

In the case of “The Seafarers,” a 1953 30-minute documentary Kubrick was commissioned to make for the Seafarers International Union, the issue became untangling the project’s rights. “We thought we knew [who owned the rights], and then somebody said, ‘Hey, by the way, I’m actually the rights holder of that,’” Chaiken chuckled, retelling the story and imagining Kubrick himself groaning at the idea that Criterion had dug it up and restored it. “ I laugh because it’s a work for hire, but at the same time, it’s a very beautiful film, and it’s his first color film, and there’s elements in it that are so Kubrickian.”

The final missing piece was the original 12-minute version of the 1951 documentary “Day of the Fight,” which Kubrick shot and edited independently for Look magazine in 1949.

“There’s a version that [Kubrick] financed and made himself, and then there’s the version that exists,” said Chaiken. “He sold the film to RKO Pictures, and they re-edited the film; they added footage that he didn’t shoot, and they changed the narration and the soundtrack that Kubrick had commissioned Gerald Fried to make.”

Kubrick’s original version had never been presented in 4K, and locating the original elements was the final piece, in Chaiken’s mind, to the set being “complete.”

“Why do you need two cuts of Day of the Fight?” Chaiken rhetorically asked before answering the question: “They are different, and to reinforce that completeness, especially with [this being] Stanley’s very first film.  His cut is actually [four minutes] shorter than the one that RKO made [and] the very first film edited and produced and directed by him.”

Chaiken’s need to present both versions of a film also applied to “The Shining,” which, after it was released domestically in May 1980 — where it bombed with critics — Kubrick spent three months re-editing, making it 25 minutes shorter, for the international release in September 1980.

What’s fascinating is that both versions have been widely available for decades; they just didn’t cross the ocean. The shorter international version is Kubrick’s final cut, and what he considered his director’s cut, never made its way to the U.S.

“[Kubrick] never pulled the U.S. theatrical version, so that version is what has existed here on video. And if you’re doing complete Kubrick, the final edit of ‘The Shining’ has to be on there. It  just has to be,” said Chaiken, noting the significant differences. “The international version is a different experience. The tone of it does shift. It does leave open the possibility that none of this is happening, and it’s all happening in Jack’s head. You really don’t understand [Jack’s] relationship with Danny in the same way in the international version.”

As IndieWire’s Jim Hemphill fully explored, the significant differences between the versions are disorienting for those who grew up loving the longer U.S. version. Chaiken anticipated what “a headscratcher” the international version would be, which is why he commissioned a new commentary track. In the new box set, the U.S. version preserves steadicam operator and founder Garrett Brown’s original Criterion commentary track, but the international version features a newly recorded track by the great Pixar animation director Lee Unkrich, who co-authored a decades-in-the-making, 1,396-page, two-volume “Stanley Kubrick’s The Shining” book, which drew from rare documents, script notes, and storyboards in the Kubrick Archive, and is based on extensive interviews with the surviving cast and crew, to tell the definitive story of the legendary and misunderstood production.

“I imagine American audiences, who love the movie, saying, ‘Why would I want to watch a shorter version of ‘The Shining’? But I think having Lee Unkrich there walking you through it and talking about some of these decisions, and talking about the production of the movie, makes it a rewarding experience,” said Chaiken. “If you’re a skeptic, it’s at least worth watching once with Lee’s commentary because it is like a master-class in ‘The Shining.’”

Twenty years after Francis Ford Coppola and Walter Murch’s groundbreaking “Apocalypse Now” sound design and roadshow ushered in surround sound, and George Lucas used the box office power of his “Star Wars” franchise to leverage theaters into adapting the new technology he was pioneering at Skywalker, Kubrick was still avoiding even doing stereo mixes of his final films.

“He stuck with mono until the end,” said Chaiken. “He was obsessed with presentation, and he truly felt, ‘I would rather have good mono than bad stereo,’ and he didn’t trust that these theaters were set up in such a way that the stereo mix.”

For the box set, Criterion does offer 5.1 mixes of Kubrick’s films, but the default audio is Kubrick’s preferred mono mix. Chaiken and Criterion audio supervisor Ryan Hullings felt it important to go back to original mono mixes, which hadn’t been touched for home video in 30 years, to do new masters for the box set. In most cases, locating the original mono mix assets was not a problem, but there were exceptions, most notably “Full Metal Jacket.”

While Warner Archives had preserved all the original assets, identifying which was the final mono mix proved difficult, explained Chaiken, “ ‘Full Metal Jacket’ was a tricky one because they were trying to convince Stanley to do it in stereo. So there was the beginning of a stereo mix.” In going through what Hullings were the final mono mixes, they didn’t conform with the 35mm print he was using as a comparison. As they did throughout the process of making the box set, the Criterion team turned to Kubrick’s surviving collaborators, in this case “Full Metal Jacket” editor Martin Hunter and Nigel Galt, who would go on to edit “Eyes Wide Shut,” but had done Foley and sound work on “Full Metal Jacket.”

According to Chaiken, it was Galt who helped them solve the puzzle, telling Hullings, “You’ll know you have the right mono mix of ‘Full Metal Jacket’ because during the end credits, in the original mono theatrical mix, we screwed up.” The final song over credits is The Rolling Stones’ “Paint It Black,” but according to Galt, ‘When it went out originally theatrically, we had it at 25 frames per second, so it’s actually sped up. So if you have the mix that has ‘Paint It Black’ at 25 frames per second, that’s it.’”

And yes, on the new Criterion, the “Full Metal Jacket” mono mix preserved “Paint It Black” playing approximately four percent faster than The Rolling Stones recorded it.

“Stanley passed away in 1999, and that was the beginning of when the DVD revolution was starting to happen,” said Chaiken. “So, the idea of Blu-ray and then 4K, you’re in a realm that Kubrick could never have imagined.”

Chaiken confronted this head-on, starting the project with “Eyes Wide Shut”—which he described as one of Kubrick’s hardest films to bridge the gap between the filmmaker’s original intention and the latest in modern home video technology. In working with “Eyes Wide Shut” cinematographer Larry Smith, Chaiken came to understand how much of a “dreamy quality” is a product of the unique way Smith and Kubrick exposed and developed the celluloid.

“It’s never going to be a one-to-one correlation between film and video; you can get close, but video is getting sharper and sharper and sharper,” said Chaiken. “I saw it with ‘Eyes Wide Shut,’ that you could go very hardcore into the 4K, and you could see details in the shadows and things that Kubrick would never have wanted you to see.”

Chaiken and Criterion worked closely with the Warner Archives, Smith, and other collaborators, going back and forth with different passes to figure out how best to find a happy medium, which, with most films, meant sometimes dialing down the HDR and leaning into “more filmic, warmer curve” with the color grade.

The more Chaiken researched, the more he learned just how obsessive Kubrick was about the presentation of his films, and admitted he would get obsessive wondering if they had gotten the colors of a particular transfer right. He sought out advice from Maria Palazzola, long-time restoration supervisor, colorist, and former transfer expert at The Criterion Collection during 1980s and ‘90s, having overseen “director approved” versions of Kubrick’s Criterion laser discs.

“I had assumed that there was an intermediary, or Leon Vitali, Stanley’s longtime assistant, helping Maria,” said Chaiken.”But when I talked to her, she said, ‘No, no, Stanley was calling me directly.’”

Chaiken said he flooded Palazzola with questions trying to find out just how obsessive Kubrick was about his home video transfers and what, in particular, he obsessed about. Chaiken had heard the stories that Kubrick had made Palazzola re-transfer films countless times.

Chaiken explained his assumptions were wrong, “She said that was absolutely not the case. That  [Kubrick] understood the limitations of the technology at that time, and he wanted them to look as good as they could possibly look on home video.”

For example, according Palazzola told Chaiken with “2001: A Space Odyssey,” Kubrick only pressed hard on two key sections.

“He wanted to be sure that the Dawn of Man sequence — that was front projected: The backgrounds were projected when they were filming it — and so he wanted to make sure that you couldn’t see any reflection in the eyes of the apes from that front projection,” said Chaiken. “And she said the one reel that he made her re-transfer a few times was the last reel, in the French boudoir, because [Kubrick] didn’t want it to be too bright.”

Chaiken said the anecdotes of Kubrick’s collaboration with Palazzola changed his perspective on his own work: “Hearing that he was relatively easy to work with was a bit of a relief because he cared about this stuff immensely, but he understood that film is not video. It’s a completely different medium.”

Chaiken gives full credit to visual artist Drusilla Adeline (aka Sister Hyde) for the design of the Kubrick box set, and in this video, you can hear them discuss how the fonts, color, layout, and the box itself were built around the theme of “Kubrick at work,” and how, in going through his archive, you get a sense of him leaving his fingerprints (literally and figuratively) on the making of his films.

One of the things that is striking about the box set, loaded with 16 different films, extras, and supporting materials, is how — compared to most box sets — it is laid out in a way that it is very easy to find what you are looking for, but also clear explanations for each film (each separated by a tab and film poster) of what is versions, audio, and extras are available. Chaiken said that in going through Kubrick’s work, it was clear what an organized and clear archivist the filmmaker was himself.

“In my mind, I was thinking about the Library of America —  what would a Library of America compendium of Stanley Kubrick look like? That  was definitely a touchstone,” said Chaiken. “Library of America editions, where they’ll [be] a single compendium of a great American author and you have the major works, but then you also have these interesting tangential pieces, and they’re laid out so beautifully, they’re just so uniform. They have the little tab that marks a page.”

DC Studios Officially Casts Mason Thames As Robin — Will He Play Him In Live-Action?

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

We might not know when the DC Universe will unveil its version of Batman, but we just got news that the franchise has cast its Robin. Well, at least we know who'll be voicing one of the Robins in the upcoming "Dynamic Duo" movie. In an interview with Collider, DC Studios co-head Peter Safran confirmed that Mason Thames has been cast as the voice of Dick Grayson in the upcoming film.

Thames is best known for starring in "The Black Phone" and its sequel, as well as playing Hiccup Horrendous Haddock III in the live-action "How to Train Your Dragon" movie. But it seems he's been gunning for a DC role. "We always talk about wanting volunteers and not recruits to be part of the DCU, to be part of DC Studios," said Safran, "and Mason always wanted to be Dick Grayson. He just did."

According to the producer, Thames came in to read for Grayson and "auditioned the s*** out of it." "He's a wonderful young actor," he continued. "But boy, did he want this. He was just obsessed. When he came over to the DC offices, he came over with his dad at the time, and we've got tons of great archives and stuff in there, and wanted to take a picture of everything and with everything. He's a fan."

That certainly bodes well for a committed performance from the 19-year-old, who most recently appeared alongside Dave Franco and O'Shea Jackson Jr. in the most startling comedy of 2026, "Idiots." The question is, will Thames actually play Dick Grayson in live-action? According to past comments from Safran's DC Studios co-head James Gunn, that's exactly what will happen. But there's no confirmation as of yet.

"Dynamic Duo" is an animated film that's being developed by DC Studios in collaboration with Swaybox Studios. The latter's unique animation style combines puppetry and CGI to create a stop-motion-like effect that will be used to tell Jason Todd and Dick Grayson's origin stories. Mason Thames' casting as the latter had previously been rumored, with scooper Jeff Sneider reporting it in September 2026. But now it's official, meaning Thames will join Logan Kim, who's playing Jason Todd, in "Dynamic Duo" — much to the dismay of "Titans" star Curran Walters, who reacted to the news of the Jason Todd recasting with disappointment.

The movie is set for a September 22, 2028 release, with Arthur Mintz, who co-founded Swaybox with Theresa Andersson in 2013, directing. For now, further details about the film are scarce, but Lee Pace and Steve Buscemi have reportedly been cast as members of the Red Hood Gang.

As it stands, we at least know who will be voicing two of the most significant versions of Robin in the DC Universe. The question is, will Thames and Kim portray their characters in live-action? They certainly should, given James Gunn's prior comments.

When he and Peter Safran became co-heads of DC Studios in 2022, they promised this latest on-screen superhero franchise would not simply be more of the same. Gunn touted a shared universe without a house style and confirmed that DC Universe actors would portray their character across film, TV, animation, and even video games, streamlining the franchise in a way that sounded as though it could be bad news for animation voice actors. But it remains to be seen whether he will stick to that claim moving forward, especially since much has changed since 2022.

James Gunn has repeated his claim about actors portraying their DC Universe characters across mediums on several occasions. In 2023, he told DC.com "Television, movies and games all intertwine within the same universe. We're going to cast actors that are going to be able to play the characters in this, as well as in other things, some of which we've already cast." Thus far, Frank Grillo has played Rick Flag Sr. on the big screen in "Superman," on TV in "Peacemaker," and voiced the character for animation in "Creature Commandos." As such, it would make sense for Logan Kim and Mason Thames to follow a similar path.

But as we've seen in recent years, nothing is set in stone. Leaving aside the Warner Bros. Discovery/Paramount merger becoming official in October 2026, James Gunn and Peter Safran announced a slate of movies back in 2022, some of which have now been scrapped. Gunn confirmed that DC had cancelled the planned "The Authority" movie, for instance, and he has spoken openly about how plans for the DC Universe otherwise shifted following Warner Bros.' initial announcement. All of which raises the question of whether he's sticking to his plan for actor continuity across mediums.

It would certainly make a lot of sense given his previous explanations. In 2023, Gunn spoke to The Hollywood Reporter about how Aaron Pierre would be playing Green Lantern John Stewart in both the HBO series "Lanterns" and on the big screen in the upcoming "Man of Tomorrow." "We don't want the series to feel in any way like step-children or lesser-than," he told the outlet. "[The show is] just another way to tell a story." Time will tell if the same holds for Kim and Thames.

Court Orders Plaintiff to File Handwritten Opposition,

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

after noting that many of plaintiff's filings contained seeming AI hallucinations, despite assertions in plaintiff's filings that all the citations were valid.

From Judge Aileen Cannon (S.D. Fla.) in Paulk v. Larva Labs, LLC, decided in June but just posted on Westlaw:

This case presents a dispute over Defendant's alleged fraudulent interference with Plaintiff's Non-Fungible Tokens ("NFTs"). Plaintiff's Complaint, initially filed in Florida state court on January 20, 2026, alleges that in June 2017, Plaintiff used a publicly available software tool to generate 141 NFTs on the Ethereum blockchain. Each of these NFTs represents ownership of a unique CryptoPunk that was part of the first version of CryptoPunks ("V1"), a pioneering generative art project consisting of 24x24 pixel portraits. To create the NFTs, Plaintiff paid only nominal "gas fees" (i.e., fees paid to third parties who validate transactions on the Ethereum blockchain), and the NFTs "have remained in [a] dormant wallet" controlled to this day by Plaintiff.

Plaintiff alleges that, on June 23, 2017, Defendant "deployed" a second version of CryptoPunks ("V2"), "claiming V1 contained a 'bug.'" The Complaint then asserts that Defendant "suppressed V1 marketplace activity while promoting V2 as the sole authoritative collection, rendering Plaintiff's assets commercially impaired". Based on these allegations, the Complaint asserts causes of action for fraudulent concealment (Count I), unjust enrichment (Count II), declaratory relief (Count III), and injunctive relief (Count IV), seeking unspecified monetary damages and an injunction.

The court discussed various procedural issues, and then added:

As a separate matter, the Court is concerned more generally about the plethora of legal filings seemingly prepared by artificial intelligence and submitted by Plaintiff that make frivolous and unsupported arguments to the Court [see, e.g., ECF Nos. 15, 16, 17, 18, 19, 26, 26-1, 31, 32, 33]. As an example, Plaintiff makes the obviously incorrect submission that the citizenship of Defendant's counsel can be considered in determining complete diversity.

And many of Plaintiff's filings contain disclaimers to the Court, expressly certifying "that every legal authority, statutory provision, rule of procedure, and historical case citation referenced within [the filings] has been independently read, analyzed, verified, and cross-referenced against official federal and state legal repositories." In each of these filings, "Plaintiff guarantees that every cited case stands as valid, binding law within the United States Supreme Court and the Eleventh Circuit Court of Appeals, possesses an active and unchecked standing, and accurately represents the procedural postures asserted." And he expressly warrants that he has not included any "fictitious or unverified citations," and that he has "manually double-checked" each case to ensure that it "support[s] Plaintiff's cause perfectly, ensuring that no unverified or 'hallucinated' data is presented to the Court."

No doubt Plaintiff's artificial intelligence legal counsel told him to say all this. And the AI certainly had the right idea—litigants should not make misrepresentations to the Court. Unfortunately, Plaintiff did not practice what his artificial counsel preached. Instead, he cites to "Snapper v. Redan, 523 U.S. 83 (1998)"—a case which does not exist—for the proposition that "when a company's chief legal representative has been sent formal notification at a publicly advertised professional office location, the entity cannot assert an administrative breakdown within its secondary commercial mail hubs as an equitable basis to vacate an otherwise valid default". Notwithstanding the made-up citation, the case Plaintiff means to cite, Snapper, Inc. v. Redan (11th Cir. 1999), is an Eleventh Circuit case that says nothing of the sort. Instead, Snapper established that federal appellate courts have jurisdiction to review remand orders based on the enforcement of contractual forum selection clauses. It does not mention vacatur, or default, or even mail—and it certainly does not stand for the extremely narrow proposition for which Plaintiff cites it.

In light of all this, Plaintiff has violated his duty of candor to the Court and has committed a Rule 11 violation…. [T]he Court [therefore] imposes filing sanctions as follows:

Plaintiff failed to respond to the motion to dismiss, and in a later opinion the court granted that motion.

Eugene Volokh is the Thomas M. Siebel Senior Fellow at the Hoover Institution at Stanford, and the Gary T. Schwartz Distinguished Professor of Law Emeritus and Distinguished Research Professor at UCLA School of Law.
